## 1. Universal Bounds On Entropy Production From Fluctuating Coarse-grained Trajectories

- **Score**: 8.5 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: Nat. Rev. Phys.  |  **Feed**: Nature Reviews Physics
- **Direction**: Information Thermodynamics
- **Published**: 2026-07-20

- **Link**: [https://www.nature.com/articles/s42254-026-00954-5](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42254-026-00954-5)
- **Tags**: `entropy production`  `stochastic thermodynamics`  `coarse-graining`  `fluctuation theorems`  `non-equilibrium`

### TL;DR

This review presents universal lower bounds on entropy production derived from coarse-grained trajectories in stochastic thermodynamics, applicable to both steady and time-dependent regimes.

### Motivation

Entropy production is a key non-equilibrium quantity but is often difficult to measure in partially observed systems, motivating the need for model-free bounds from accessible observables.

### Method

The paper reviews stochastic thermodynamics approaches that infer entropy production bounds from coarse-grained observables such as states, currents, correlations, and waiting times, without requiring full microscopic knowledge.

### Result

It highlights universal bounds that hold across various regimes and discusses progress in trajectory-level estimation of entropy production.

### Conclusion

The review consolidates recent advances in estimating entropy production from coarse-grained data, providing a framework for characterizing non-equilibrium behavior in complex systems.

*Keywords: entropy production, stochastic thermodynamics, coarse-graining, fluctuation theorems, non-equilibrium*

## 7. Bottlenecks In Quantum Channels And Finite Temperature Phases Of Matter

- **Source**: Phys. Rev. Lett.  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Letters
- **Direction**: Quantum Information
- **Published**: 2026-07-28
- **Authors**: Tibor Rakovszky, Benedikt Placke, Nikolas P. Breuckmann, And Vedika Khemani
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/y9z2-52rl](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/y9z2-52rl)
- **Tags**: `bottleneck theorem`  `quantum channels`  `spectral gap`  `finite-temperature phases`  `Markovian dynamics`

### TL;DR

This paper proves a quantum analog of the bottleneck theorem for Markovian channels, linking spectral gaps to steady-state weights and applying it to finite-temperature phases of matter.

### Motivation

The bottleneck theorem is a fundamental tool for classical Markov chains, but its quantum analog was missing. Understanding bottlenecks in quantum channels is crucial for characterizing mixing times and relaxation dynamics in quantum systems.

### Method

The authors prove a quantum bottleneck theorem for Markovian quantum channels, showing that if two subspaces are separated by a region of low steady-state weight, then transitions between them are suppressed. They then apply this to finite-temperature phases, using it to bound relaxation times.

### Result

They establish a rigorous connection between the spectral gap of a quantum channel and the steady-state weights of subspaces, and demonstrate that this can be used to prove slow relaxation in certain finite-temperature phases, such as symmetry-broken phases.

### Conclusion

The work provides a new tool for analyzing quantum channels and contributes to understanding the dynamical stability of quantum phases of matter, with implications for quantum information and thermodynamics.

*Keywords: bottleneck theorem, quantum channels, spectral gap, finite-temperature phases, Markovian dynamics*

### Motivation

### Method

They provide rigorous proofs and constructions demonstrating that circuits with anticoncentration can be efficiently converted into approximate 2-designs with minimal overhead.

### Result

They prove that anticoncentration implies approximate 2-design up to logarithmic factors in depth, and provide explicit circuit constructions achieving this.

### Conclusion

Anticoncentration is almost as powerful as 2-designs for practical purposes, simplifying the requirements for quantum information protocols.

- **Tags**: `noise`  `qubit`  `dynamical stabilizer codes`  `measurement-biased noise`  `teraquop volume`

### TL;DR

This paper optimizes measurement schedules for dynamical color codes under measurement-biased noise, introducing the teraquop volume metric and showing that belief matching decoding can significantly improve performance.

### Motivation

Dynamical stabilizer codes may enable large-scale quantum computation, but their performance depends on the measurement schedule, especially when measurements are noisy. The authors aim to optimize schedules for hardware with noisy readouts.

### Method

They analyze dynamically condensed color codes under various noise biases, using the teraquop volume (product of qubits and measurement rounds) to compare performance. They test repeating measurements and compare belief matching with minimum-weight perfect matching decoders.

### Result

Repeating measurements improves performance under measurement-biased noise but offers little benefit for unbiased or X-biased noise. The teraquop volume reveals that differences are mainly due to measurement rounds, not qubit count. Belief matching can turn a worst-performing code into a best-performing one.

### Conclusion

The teraquop volume is a valuable metric for comparing codes, and correlated-error decoding is crucial for optimizing dynamical codes under realistic noise.

*Keywords: dynamical stabilizer codes, measurement-biased noise, teraquop volume, belief matching, quantum error correction*

The number of T gates is a critical resource for fault-tolerant quantum computing, and understanding the minimal T-count for state preparation is fundamental for efficient quantum algorithms.

The authors improve prior work by Low, Kliuchnikov, and Schaeffer, providing constructive algorithms and lower bounds to establish the optimal asymptotic scaling of T-count for state preparation and diagonal unitary synthesis.

### TL;DR

This paper characterizes the minimum number of two-qubit Clifford operations (CZ-complexity) needed to prepare graph states, relating it to rank-width and providing algorithms for interval and circle graphs.

### Motivation

Understanding the resource cost of preparing graph states is crucial for quantum information processing, as graph states are key resources for measurement-based quantum computing and quantum error correction.

### Method

The authors define CZ-complexity and provide a combinatorial characterization of graph-state transformations using vertex deletions, local complementations, and edge-complementations. They then relate CZ-complexity to rank-width and develop preparation algorithms for specific graph classes.

### Result

They show that for any graph with n vertices and rank-width r, the CZ-complexity is O(n*r) and at least Ω(n/r) for connected graphs, with bounds close to optimal. They also give algorithms with CZ-complexity O(n) for interval graphs and O(n log n) for circle graphs.

### Conclusion

The work provides a deeper understanding of the complexity of graph-state preparation, offering both lower and upper bounds and efficient algorithms for certain graph families, which may inform practical quantum circuit optimization.

*Keywords: graph states, Clifford circuits, rank-width, quantum circuit complexity, local complementation*

- **Tags**: `noise`  `disorder`  `qubit`  `decoder`  `decodability`

### TL;DR

This paper introduces a partition function framework to estimate logical error rates in stabilizer codes, connecting decoding success to statistical mechanics and demonstrating improved sample efficiency.

### Motivation

Accurate estimation of logical error rates is crucial for assessing quantum error correction performance, but direct simulation of decoders can be inefficient, especially at low noise.

### Method

The authors define a ratio of partition functions corresponding to maximum likelihood decoding and an order probability for a probabilistic decoder, mapping to disordered statistical mechanics models. They analyze the toric code under bitflip noise and the color code under various noise models.

### Result

They show that estimating decoding probability and order probability is more sample efficient than counting decoder failures, and discuss the possibility of a decodability boundary distinct from the order-disorder phase boundary. Ensembling remains useful even when noise lifts degeneracy.

### Conclusion

The partition function approach provides a powerful tool for estimating logical error rates and understanding decoding strategies, with implications for optimizing quantum error correction.

*Keywords: stabilizer codes, partition function, maximum likelihood decoding, toric code, statistical mechanics*

### TL;DR

This paper introduces Shadow Pauli Flow, a new characterization of robust determinism in measurement-based quantum computing with Pauli measurements, proving it necessary and sufficient and computable in polynomial time.

### Motivation

Pauli Flow was previously shown to be insufficient for characterizing determinism in MBQC with Pauli measurements, and the authors aim to provide a complete characterization.

### Method

The authors define Shadow Pauli Flow as an extension of Pauli Flow and prove that an MBQC is robustly deterministic if and only if its correction strategy is consistent with a Shadow Pauli flow, also providing a polynomial-time algorithm.

### Result

They demonstrate that Pauli flow is necessary for robust determinism in a weaker sense, but Shadow Pauli Flow is necessary and sufficient in full generality, and can be computed in polynomial time.

### Conclusion

Shadow Pauli Flow provides a complete and efficient characterization of determinism in MBQCs with Pauli measurements, advancing the theory of measurement-based quantum computing.

*Keywords: Measurement-Based Quantum Computing, Pauli Flow, Determinism, Graph States, Quantum Computation*

Derives a complete analytical solution for two emitters and uses a numerically exact method for larger ensembles, characterizing the transition from Markovian to non-Markovian dynamics.

Identifies three regimes: Markovian superradiance, non-Markovian superabsorption, and critical pulsed emission; shows that the critical spectral width increases with emitter number and that superradiant scaling degrades to subquadratic in the perfect cavity limit.
